# The Commandments of Jesus Christ

> A Scripture-embedded, source-rich study edition of Jesus' commands in Matthew, Mark, Luke, John, and Revelation.

**Edition date:** 2026-07-31  
**Catalog:** 83 consolidated command families: 70 from the four Gospels and 13 from Revelation.    
**Embedded Bible text:** World English Bible (WEB), a public-domain translation.  

## What this edition corrects

The earlier study edition preserved the command catalog and commentary but left the reader to look up nearly every biblical and historical source. This revision embeds the actual cited biblical passages, replaces bare patristic references with substantive excerpts, adds direct public-domain excerpts from the Lutheran Confessions, and supplies study questions and prayers for sustained theological and devotional use.

## Translation and quotation policy

- The complete verse units cited by each command family are printed from the **World English Bible (WEB)**. The WEB is used instead of the ESV so this file and its PDF may be stored and shared publicly without reproducing a copyrighted translation beyond its permissions.
- Readers may compare every canonical locator with an owned or licensed ESV. No TLSB or other copyrighted study-note text is reproduced.
- Early Christian and Lutheran excerpts are from public-domain English translations. Spelling, capitalization, punctuation, and archaic pronouns are lightly modernized for readability; citations are retained so the underlying editions can be checked.
- Scripture remains the sole norm. Patristic and confessional texts are historical and theological witnesses, not additions to the canon.

## How to use a command-family study

1. Read every embedded passage before the commentary.
2. Distinguish speaker, audience, literary setting, enduring principle, and context-bound direction.
3. Read the Law/Gospel synthesis: the command exposes sin and directs the baptized life, while Christ alone justifies and supplies what He commands.
4. Compare the actual early-Christian and confessional excerpts.
5. Bring difficult applications to a faithful pastor.

# Command-family studies

## Repentance, Faith, and Trust

Christ calls sinners away from unbelief and self-reliance to the Father's mercy in Him.

<a id="command-001"></a>

### 1. Repent and believe the Gospel

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #1, #2  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 4:17; 11:20-21; 12:41; Mark 1:15; Luke 5:32; 10:13; 11:32; 13:3,5; 15:7,10; 16:30; 17:3-4; 24:47

#### Command-family wording

> Turn from sin to God and trust the good news of the kingdom in Christ.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 4:17

> **17** From that time, Jesus began to preach, and to say, “Repent! For the Kingdom of Heaven is at hand.”

##### Matthew 11:20-21

> **20** Then he began to denounce the cities in which most of his mighty works had been done, because they didn’t repent.
>
> **21** “Woe to you, Chorazin! Woe to you, Bethsaida! For if the mighty works had been done in Tyre and Sidon which were done in you, they would have repented long ago in sackcloth and ashes.

##### Matthew 12:41

> **41** The men of Nineveh will stand up in the judgment with this generation and will condemn it, for they repented at the preaching of Jonah; and behold, someone greater than Jonah is here.

##### Mark 1:15

> **15** and saying, “The time is fulfilled, and God’s Kingdom is at hand! Repent, and believe in the Good News.”

##### Luke 5:32

> **32** I have not come to call the righteous, but sinners, to repentance.”

##### Luke 10:13

> **13** “Woe to you, Chorazin! Woe to you, Bethsaida! For if the mighty works had been done in Tyre and Sidon which were done in you, they would have repented long ago, sitting in sackcloth and ashes.

##### Luke 11:32

> **32** The men of Nineveh will stand up in the judgment with this generation, and will condemn it, for they repented at the preaching of Jonah; and behold, one greater than Jonah is here.

##### Luke 13:3,5

> **3** I tell you, no, but unless you repent, you will all perish in the same way.
>
> **5** I tell you, no, but, unless you repent, you will all perish in the same way.”

##### Luke 15:7,10

> **7** I tell you that even so there will be more joy in heaven over one sinner who repents, than over ninety-nine righteous people who need no repentance.
>
> **10** Even so, I tell you, there is joy in the presence of the angels of God over one sinner repenting.”

##### Luke 16:30

> **30** “He said, ‘No, father Abraham, but if one goes to them from the dead, they will repent.’

##### Luke 17:3-4

> **3** Be careful. If your brother sins against you, rebuke him. If he repents, forgive him.
>
> **4** If he sins against you seven times in the day, and seven times returns, saying, ‘I repent,’ you shall forgive him.”

##### Luke 24:47

> **47** and that repentance and remission of sins should be preached in his name to all the nations, beginning at Jerusalem.

#### Reading the passages together

- Repentance is God-given contrition joined to faith in Christ's forgiveness, not self-repair or a meritorious work.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Repentance is God-given contrition joined to faith in Christ's forgiveness, not self-repair or a meritorious work. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Didache 4.14 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> In church you shall acknowledge your transgressions, and you shall not come near for your prayer with an evil conscience. This is the way of life.

*Study significance:* Repentance is concrete, ecclesial, and joined to prayer rather than reduced to vague regret.

**Augustine, Confessions 10.29 (Pusey translation, lightly modernized)**

> Give what You command, and command what You will.

*Study significance:* Augustine confesses that divine commands expose need and that God must supply the grace by which they are obeyed.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Augsburg Confession XII**

> Properly speaking, repentance consists of these two parts: one is contrition, that is, terrors smiting the conscience through the knowledge of sin; the other is faith, born of the Gospel or of absolution, which believes that for Christ's sake sins are forgiven, comforts the conscience, and delivers it from terrors.

*Study significance:* Repentance includes both truthful sorrow over sin and faith in Christ's forgiveness.

**Augsburg Confession IV**

> People cannot be justified before God by their own strength, merits, or works, but are freely justified for Christ's sake, through faith, when they believe that they are received into favor and that their sins are forgiven for Christ's sake.

*Study significance:* The command must never be turned into a competing basis of justification.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV-V (justification and the ministry of the Word); Augsburg Confession XII (repentance); Small Catechism: Creed, Baptism, and Confession; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration V-VI (Law and Gospel; third use of the Law).

#### Practicing the command

Confess concrete sins, receive absolution, believe the Gospel, and bear fruits fitting repentance.

#### Misuse caution

Do not reduce repentance to regret, a one-time decision, or moral improvement without faith.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **repent and believe the gospel** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to repent and believe the gospel. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

### 2. Believe in God and in Christ; walk in the Light

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #3, #4, #5, #6  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 14:31; 21:21-22; Mark 11:22-24; Luke 17:6; John 3:16-18; 6:29,35; 7:38; 11:25-26; 12:35-36,44-46; 14:1,11; 20:27,31

#### Command-family wording

> Trust the Father and the Son, reject unbelief, and walk while Christ the Light is given.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 14:31

> **31** Immediately Jesus stretched out his hand, took hold of him, and said to him, “You of little faith, why did you doubt?”

##### Matthew 21:21-22

> **21** Jesus answered them, “Most certainly I tell you, if you have faith and don’t doubt, you will not only do what was done to the fig tree, but even if you told this mountain, ‘Be taken up and cast into the sea,’ it would be done.
>
> **22** All things, whatever you ask in prayer, believing, you will receive.”

##### Mark 11:22-24

> **22** Jesus answered them, “Have faith in God.
>
> **23** For most certainly I tell you, whoever may tell this mountain, ‘Be taken up and cast into the sea,’ and doesn’t doubt in his heart, but believes that what he says is happening, he shall have whatever he says.
>
> **24** Therefore I tell you, all things whatever you pray and ask for, believe that you have received them, and you shall have them.

##### Luke 17:6

> **6** The Lord said, “If you had faith like a grain of mustard seed, you would tell this sycamore tree, ‘Be uprooted and be planted in the sea,’ and it would obey you.

##### John 3:16-18

> **16** For God so loved the world, that he gave his only born Son, that whoever believes in him should not perish, but have eternal life.
>
> **17** For God didn’t send his Son into the world to judge the world, but that the world should be saved through him.
>
> **18** He who believes in him is not judged. He who doesn’t believe has been judged already, because he has not believed in the name of the only born Son of God.

##### John 6:29,35

> **29** Jesus answered them, “This is the work of God, that you believe in him whom he has sent.”
>
> **35** Jesus said to them, “I am the bread of life. Whoever comes to me will not be hungry, and whoever believes in me will never be thirsty.

##### John 7:38

> **38** He who believes in me, as the Scripture has said, from within him will flow rivers of living water.”

##### John 11:25-26

> **25** Jesus said to her, “I am the resurrection and the life. He who believes in me will still live, even if he dies.
>
> **26** Whoever lives and believes in me will never die. Do you believe this?”

##### John 12:35-36,44-46

> **35** Jesus therefore said to them, “Yet a little while the light is with you. Walk while you have the light, that darkness doesn’t overtake you. He who walks in the darkness doesn’t know where he is going.
>
> **36** While you have the light, believe in the light, that you may become children of light.” Jesus said these things, and he departed and hid himself from them.
>
> **44** Jesus cried out and said, “Whoever believes in me, believes not in me, but in him who sent me.
>
> **45** He who sees me sees him who sent me.
>
> **46** I have come as a light into the world, that whoever believes in me may not remain in the darkness.

##### John 14:1,11

> **1** “Don’t let your heart be troubled. Believe in God. Believe also in me.
>
> **11** Believe me that I am in the Father, and the Father in me; or else believe me for the very works’ sake.

##### John 20:27,31

> **27** Then he said to Thomas, “Reach here your finger, and see my hands. Reach here your hand, and put it into my side. Don’t be unbelieving, but believing.”
>
> **31** but these are written that you may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, and that believing you may have life in his name.

#### Reading the passages together

- Biblical faith receives Christ and His promises; the Word creates the faith it requires and directs frightened consciences away from self-confidence.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Biblical faith receives Christ and His promises; the Word creates the faith it requires and directs frightened consciences away from self-confidence. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Augustine, Confessions 10.29 (Pusey translation, lightly modernized)**

> Give what You command, and command what You will.

*Study significance:* Augustine confesses that divine commands expose need and that God must supply the grace by which they are obeyed.

**Justin Martyr, First Apology 67 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> On the day called Sunday, all who live in cities or in the country gather together to one place, and the memoirs of the apostles or the writings of the prophets are read as long as time permits.

*Study significance:* The church gathers around apostolic and prophetic Scripture rather than private inspiration alone.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Augsburg Confession IV**

> People cannot be justified before God by their own strength, merits, or works, but are freely justified for Christ's sake, through faith, when they believe that they are received into favor and that their sins are forgiven for Christ's sake.

*Study significance:* The command must never be turned into a competing basis of justification.

**Augsburg Confession V**

> That we may obtain this faith, the Ministry of Teaching the Gospel and administering the Sacraments was instituted. Through the Word and Sacraments, as through instruments, the Holy Spirit is given, who works faith where and when it pleases God.

*Study significance:* Faith is created and sustained through Christ's appointed means rather than self-generated resolve.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV-V (justification and the ministry of the Word); Augsburg Confession XII (repentance); Small Catechism: Creed, Baptism, and Confession; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration V-VI (Law and Gospel; third use of the Law).

#### Practicing the command

Attend to preaching and Scripture, pray from Christ's promises, and bring doubt into the fellowship of the Church.

#### Misuse caution

Prayer promises are governed by God's will and are not blank checks for prosperity.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **believe in god and in christ; walk in the light** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to believe in god and in christ; walk in the light. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

<a id="command-003"></a>

### 3. Receive the new birth from above

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #7  
**Canonical passages:** John 3:3-8

#### Command-family wording

> Receive the Spirit-given birth from above rather than presuming on ancestry or achievement.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### John 3:3-8

> **3** Jesus answered him, “Most certainly I tell you, unless one is born anew, he can’t see God’s Kingdom.”
>
> **4** Nicodemus said to him, “How can a man be born when he is old? Can he enter a second time into his mother’s womb and be born?”
>
> **5** Jesus answered, “Most certainly I tell you, unless one is born of water and Spirit, he can’t enter into God’s Kingdom.
>
> **6** That which is born of the flesh is flesh. That which is born of the Spirit is spirit.
>
> **7** Don’t marvel that I said to you, ‘You must be born anew.’
>
> **8** The wind blows where it wants to, and you hear its sound, but don’t know where it comes from and where it is going. So is everyone who is born of the Spirit.”

#### Reading the passages together

- New birth is God's work by water and Spirit, received in faith, and therefore gift before renewed living.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

New birth is God's work by water and Spirit, received in faith, and therefore gift before renewed living. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Didache 7.1 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> Concerning baptism, baptize thus: Having first rehearsed all these things, baptize in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, in living water.

*Study significance:* The early church explicitly connects catechesis, water, and the Triune name.

**Justin Martyr, First Apology 61 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> Then they are brought by us where there is water and are regenerated in the same manner in which we were ourselves regenerated: in the name of God, the Father and Lord of the universe, and of our Savior Jesus Christ, and of the Holy Spirit.

*Study significance:* This second-century description witnesses to baptism with water and the Triune name.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Small Catechism, Baptism III**

> It is not the water indeed that does these things, but the word of God which is in and with the water, and faith which trusts this word of God in the water.

*Study significance:* Baptism is God's act through water joined to His word and promise.

**Small Catechism, Baptism IV**

> The Old Adam in us should, by daily contrition and repentance, be drowned and die with all sins and evil lusts, and a new person daily come forth and arise, who shall live before God in righteousness and purity forever.

*Study significance:* The baptized life is daily repentance and new life, not a one-time memory.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV-V (justification and the ministry of the Word); Augsburg Confession XII (repentance); Small Catechism: Creed, Baptism, and Confession; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration V-VI (Law and Gospel; third use of the Law).

#### Practicing the command

Remember and use Baptism daily; hear the Spirit-bearing Word; put the old Adam to death through contrition.

#### Misuse caution

Do not turn the saying into a demand to manufacture a particular emotional conversion experience.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **receive the new birth from above** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to receive the new birth from above. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

<a id="command-004"></a>

### 4. Do not be anxious about life or tomorrow

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal with situational applications  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #8, #9  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 6:25-34; Luke 12:22-31; Matthew 10:19; Mark 13:11; Luke 12:11-12

#### Command-family wording

> Refuse consuming anxiety; seek the Father's kingdom and entrust bodily needs and future testimony to Him.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 6:25-34

> **25** Therefore I tell you, don’t be anxious for your life: what you will eat, or what you will drink; nor yet for your body, what you will wear. Isn’t life more than food, and the body more than clothing?
>
> **26** See the birds of the sky, that they don’t sow, neither do they reap, nor gather into barns. Your heavenly Father feeds them. Aren’t you of much more value than they?
>
> **27** “Which of you by being anxious, can add one moment to his lifespan?
>
> **28** Why are you anxious about clothing? Consider the lilies of the field, how they grow. They don’t toil, neither do they spin,
>
> **29** yet I tell you that even Solomon in all his glory was not dressed like one of these.
>
> **30** But if God so clothes the grass of the field, which today exists and tomorrow is thrown into the oven, won’t he much more clothe you, you of little faith?
>
> **31** “Therefore don’t be anxious, saying, ‘What will we eat?’, ‘What will we drink?’ or, ‘With what will we be clothed?’
>
> **32** For the Gentiles seek after all these things; for your heavenly Father knows that you need all these things.
>
> **33** But seek first God’s Kingdom and his righteousness; and all these things will be given to you as well.
>
> **34** Therefore don’t be anxious for tomorrow, for tomorrow will be anxious for itself. Each day’s own evil is sufficient.

##### Luke 12:22-31

> **22** He said to his disciples, “Therefore I tell you, don’t be anxious for your life, what you will eat, nor yet for your body, what you will wear.
>
> **23** Life is more than food, and the body is more than clothing.
>
> **24** Consider the ravens: they don’t sow, they don’t reap, they have no warehouse or barn, and God feeds them. How much more valuable are you than birds!
>
> **25** Which of you by being anxious can add a cubit to his height?
>
> **26** If then you aren’t able to do even the least things, why are you anxious about the rest?
>
> **27** Consider the lilies, how they grow. They don’t toil, neither do they spin; yet I tell you, even Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these.
>
> **28** But if this is how God clothes the grass in the field, which today exists and tomorrow is cast into the oven, how much more will he clothe you, O you of little faith?
>
> **29** “Don’t seek what you will eat or what you will drink; neither be anxious.
>
> **30** For the nations of the world seek after all of these things, but your Father knows that you need these things.
>
> **31** But seek God’s Kingdom, and all these things will be added to you.

##### Matthew 10:19

> **19** But when they deliver you up, don’t be anxious how or what you will say, for it will be given you in that hour what you will say.

##### Mark 13:11

> **11** When they lead you away and deliver you up, don’t be anxious beforehand or premeditate what you will say, but say whatever will be given you in that hour. For it is not you who speak, but the Holy Spirit.

##### Luke 12:11-12

> **11** When they bring you before the synagogues, the rulers, and the authorities, don’t be anxious how or what you will answer or what you will say;
>
> **12** for the Holy Spirit will teach you in that same hour what you must say.”

#### Reading the passages together

- Jesus forbids unbelieving anxiety, not prudent planning, work, treatment, or honest lament.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Jesus forbids unbelieving anxiety, not prudent planning, work, treatment, or honest lament. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Augustine, Confessions 1.1 (Pusey translation, lightly modernized)**

> You have made us for Yourself, and our heart is restless until it rests in You.

*Study significance:* The line helps distinguish Christ's promised rest from mere favorable circumstances.

**Didache 8.2-3 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> Neither pray as the hypocrites, but as the Lord commanded in his Gospel, thus pray: Our Father, who art in heaven. Three times in the day pray so.

*Study significance:* Jesus' model prayer was received as a repeated, formative practice rather than empty repetition.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Small Catechism, First Commandment**

> We should fear, love, and trust in God above all things.

*Study significance:* Every command family is finally ordered under trust in the true God.

**Small Catechism, Lord's Prayer, Introduction**

> God would thereby tenderly invite us to believe that He is our true Father and that we are His true children, so that we may ask Him confidently with all assurance, as dear children ask their dear father.

*Study significance:* Christian prayer rests on the Father's promise rather than quantity of words or emotional intensity.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV-V (justification and the ministry of the Word); Augsburg Confession XII (repentance); Small Catechism: Creed, Baptism, and Confession; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration V-VI (Law and Gospel; third use of the Law).

#### Practicing the command

Plan responsibly, ask for daily bread, seek appropriate help, and deliberately hand tomorrow to God.

#### Misuse caution

Never weaponize this command against people with anxiety disorders or use it to excuse negligence.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **do not be anxious about life or tomorrow** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to do not be anxious about life or tomorrow. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

### 5. Let not your heart be troubled; take courage

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #10  
**Canonical passages:** John 14:1,27; 16:33

#### Command-family wording

> Rest in Christ's peace and take courage because He has overcome the world.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### John 14:1,27

> **1** “Don’t let your heart be troubled. Believe in God. Believe also in me.
>
> **27** Peace I leave with you. My peace I give to you; not as the world gives, I give to you. Don’t let your heart be troubled, neither let it be fearful.

##### John 16:33

> **33** I have told you these things, that in me you may have peace. In the world you have trouble; but cheer up! I have overcome the world.”

#### Reading the passages together

- Christ gives peace with God through His cross and resurrection, not emotional invulnerability or favorable circumstances.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Christ gives peace with God through His cross and resurrection, not emotional invulnerability or favorable circumstances. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Augustine, Confessions 1.1 (Pusey translation, lightly modernized)**

> You have made us for Yourself, and our heart is restless until it rests in You.

*Study significance:* The line helps distinguish Christ's promised rest from mere favorable circumstances.

**1 Clement 13.1-2 (Lightfoot/Roberts-Donaldson tradition, lightly modernized)**

> Let us be humble-minded, brethren, laying aside all haughtiness and pride and foolishness and angry feelings, especially remembering the words of the Lord Jesus: Be merciful, that you may obtain mercy; forgive, that it may be forgiven unto you.

*Study significance:* Clement explicitly uses Jesus' words to form a peaceful and humble congregation.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Augsburg Confession IV**

> People cannot be justified before God by their own strength, merits, or works, but are freely justified for Christ's sake, through faith, when they believe that they are received into favor and that their sins are forgiven for Christ's sake.

*Study significance:* The command must never be turned into a competing basis of justification.

**Small Catechism, Lord's Prayer, Introduction**

> God would thereby tenderly invite us to believe that He is our true Father and that we are His true children, so that we may ask Him confidently with all assurance, as dear children ask their dear father.

*Study significance:* Christian prayer rests on the Father's promise rather than quantity of words or emotional intensity.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV-V (justification and the ministry of the Word); Augsburg Confession XII (repentance); Small Catechism: Creed, Baptism, and Confession; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration V-VI (Law and Gospel; third use of the Law).

#### Practicing the command

Use Christ's promises, receive absolution and the Supper, and comfort others with the objective Gospel.

#### Misuse caution

Grief, trauma, and clinical distress are not automatically failures to obey.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **let not your heart be troubled; take courage** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to let not your heart be troubled; take courage. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

### 6. Fear God rather than those who can kill the body

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal, especially under persecution  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #11  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 10:26-33; Luke 12:4-7

#### Command-family wording

> Do not let human threats silence confession; fear and trust the Lord who cares for body and soul.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 10:26-33

> **26** Therefore don’t be afraid of them, for there is nothing covered that will not be revealed, or hidden that will not be known.
>
> **27** What I tell you in the darkness, speak in the light; and what you hear whispered in the ear, proclaim on the housetops.
>
> **28** Don’t be afraid of those who kill the body, but are not able to kill the soul. Rather, fear him who is able to destroy both soul and body in Gehenna.
>
> **29** “Aren’t two sparrows sold for an assarion coin? Not one of them falls to the ground apart from your Father’s will.
>
> **30** But the very hairs of your head are all numbered.
>
> **31** Therefore don’t be afraid. You are of more value than many sparrows.
>
> **32** Everyone therefore who confesses me before men, I will also confess him before my Father who is in heaven.
>
> **33** But whoever denies me before men, I will also deny him before my Father who is in heaven.

##### Luke 12:4-7

> **4** “I tell you, my friends, don’t be afraid of those who kill the body, and after that have no more that they can do.
>
> **5** But I will warn you whom you should fear. Fear him who after he has killed, has power to cast into Gehenna. Yes, I tell you, fear him.
>
> **6** “Aren’t five sparrows sold for two assaria coins? Not one of them is forgotten by God.
>
> **7** But the very hairs of your head are all counted. Therefore don’t be afraid. You are of more value than many sparrows.

#### Reading the passages together

- Holy fear values God's verdict above approval or bodily safety while resting in His fatherly care.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Holy fear values God's verdict above approval or bodily safety while resting in His fatherly care. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Didache 16.1 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> Watch for your life's sake. Let not your lamps be quenched, nor your loins unloosed; but be ready, for you do not know the hour in which our Lord comes.

*Study significance:* The passage directly preserves the church's early reception of Jesus' readiness language.

**1 Clement 13.1-2 (Lightfoot/Roberts-Donaldson tradition, lightly modernized)**

> Let us be humble-minded, brethren, laying aside all haughtiness and pride and foolishness and angry feelings, especially remembering the words of the Lord Jesus: Be merciful, that you may obtain mercy; forgive, that it may be forgiven unto you.

*Study significance:* Clement explicitly uses Jesus' words to form a peaceful and humble congregation.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Small Catechism, First Commandment**

> We should fear, love, and trust in God above all things.

*Study significance:* Every command family is finally ordered under trust in the true God.

**Augsburg Confession IV**

> People cannot be justified before God by their own strength, merits, or works, but are freely justified for Christ's sake, through faith, when they believe that they are received into favor and that their sins are forgiven for Christ's sake.

*Study significance:* The command must never be turned into a competing basis of justification.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV-V (justification and the ministry of the Word); Augsburg Confession XII (repentance); Small Catechism: Creed, Baptism, and Confession; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration V-VI (Law and Gospel; third use of the Law).

#### Practicing the command

Confess Christ truthfully, refuse coercion into sin, pray for persecuted believers, and use lawful protections.

#### Misuse caution

The command does not authorize reckless provocation or contempt for bodily life.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **fear god rather than those who can kill the body** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

## Discipleship and Union with Christ

The disciple lives from Christ, follows His voice, and bears fruit through communion with Him.

<a id="command-007"></a>

### 7. Follow Jesus

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal call expressed through particular vocations  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #12  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 4:19; 8:22; 9:9; 16:24; 19:21; Mark 1:17; 2:14; 8:34; 10:21; Luke 5:27; 9:23,59; 18:22; John 1:43; 10:27; 12:26; 21:19,22

#### Command-family wording

> Hear Christ's call, belong to Him, and walk where His Word leads.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 4:19

> **19** He said to them, “Come after me, and I will make you fishers for men.”

##### Matthew 8:22

> **22** But Jesus said to him, “Follow me, and leave the dead to bury their own dead.”

##### Matthew 9:9

> **9** As Jesus passed by from there, he saw a man called Matthew sitting at the tax collection office. He said to him, “Follow me.” He got up and followed him.

##### Matthew 16:24

> **24** Then Jesus said to his disciples, “If anyone desires to come after me, let him deny himself, take up his cross, and follow me.

##### Matthew 19:21

> **21** Jesus said to him, “If you want to be perfect, go, sell what you have, and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven; and come, follow me.”

##### Mark 1:17

> **17** Jesus said to them, “Come after me, and I will make you into fishers for men.”

##### Mark 2:14

> **14** As he passed by, he saw Levi the son of Alphaeus sitting at the tax office. He said to him, “Follow me.” And he arose and followed him.

##### Mark 8:34

> **34** He called the multitude to himself with his disciples and said to them, “Whoever wants to come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me.

##### Mark 10:21

> **21** Jesus looking at him loved him, and said to him, “One thing you lack. Go, sell whatever you have and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven; and come, follow me, taking up the cross.”

##### Luke 5:27

> **27** After these things he went out and saw a tax collector named Levi sitting at the tax office, and said to him, “Follow me!”

##### Luke 9:23,59

> **23** He said to all, “If anyone desires to come after me, let him deny himself, take up his cross, and follow me.
>
> **59** He said to another, “Follow me!” But he said, “Lord, allow me first to go and bury my father.”

##### Luke 18:22

> **22** When Jesus heard these things, he said to him, “You still lack one thing. Sell all that you have and distribute it to the poor. Then you will have treasure in heaven; then come, follow me.”

##### John 1:43

> **43** On the next day, he was determined to go out into Galilee, and he found Philip. Jesus said to him, “Follow me.”

##### John 10:27

> **27** My sheep hear my voice, and I know them, and they follow me.

##### John 12:26

> **26** If anyone serves me, let him follow me. Where I am, there my servant will also be. If anyone serves me, the Father will honor him.

##### John 21:19,22

> **19** Now he said this, signifying by what kind of death he would glorify God. When he had said this, he said to him, “Follow me.”
>
> **22** Jesus said to him, “If I desire that he stay until I come, what is that to you? You follow me.”

#### Reading the passages together

- Following begins with the Shepherd's call and gift; particular narratives show different outward costs.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Following begins with the Shepherd's call and gift; particular narratives show different outward costs. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Didache 1.1-2 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> There are two ways, one of life and one of death; but there is a great difference between these two ways. The way of life, then, is this: First, you shall love God who made you; second, your neighbor as yourself; and all things whatsoever you would not have done to you, do not do to another.

*Study significance:* An early Christian catechetical summary joins love of God, love of neighbor, and concrete conduct.

**1 Clement 49.1 (Lightfoot translation, lightly modernized)**

> Let the one who has love in Christ keep the commandments of Christ.

*Study significance:* The sentence binds Christian love to Christ's actual commands rather than sentiment alone.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Small Catechism, First Commandment**

> We should fear, love, and trust in God above all things.

*Study significance:* Every command family is finally ordered under trust in the true God.

**Augsburg Confession VI**

> This faith is bound to bring forth good fruits, and it is necessary to do good works commanded by God because of God's will; yet we should not rely on those works to merit justification before God.

*Study significance:* Good works are necessary fruits of faith, not merits that purchase God's favor.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV and VI (faith and new obedience); Apology of the Augsburg Confession IV (faith and fruits); Small Catechism: First Commandment and Baptism IV; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration IV and VI (good works and the Law).

#### Practicing the command

Learn from the Gospels and order every vocation, relationship, and decision under Christ.

#### Misuse caution

Not every command to an individual disciple establishes identical outward action for every Christian.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **follow jesus** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to follow jesus. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 10:38

> **38** He who doesn’t take his cross and follow after me isn’t worthy of me.

##### Matthew 16:24

> **24** Then Jesus said to his disciples, “If anyone desires to come after me, let him deny himself, take up his cross, and follow me.

##### Mark 8:34

> **34** He called the multitude to himself with his disciples and said to them, “Whoever wants to come after me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow me.

#### Reading the passages together

- Self-denial surrenders the self as lord; the cross is suffering borne because one belongs to Christ, not pain chosen for its own sake.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Didache 1.1-2 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> There are two ways, one of life and one of death; but there is a great difference between these two ways. The way of life, then, is this: First, you shall love God who made you; second, your neighbor as yourself; and all things whatsoever you would not have done to you, do not do to another.

*Study significance:* An early Christian catechetical summary joins love of God, love of neighbor, and concrete conduct.

**Augustine, Confessions 10.29 (Pusey translation, lightly modernized)**

> People cannot be justified before God by their own strength, merits, or works, but are freely justified for Christ's sake, through faith, when they believe that they are received into favor and that their sins are forgiven for Christ's sake.

Say no to desires contrary to God's Word, endure faithful costs, support sufferers, and seek help in abuse.

#### Misuse caution

### 9. Abide in Christ, remain in His love, and bear fruit

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #13, #14, #91  
**Canonical passages:** John 15:4-10,16

#### Command-family wording

> Remain in Christ so that His life bears enduring fruit through you.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### John 15:4-10,16

> **4** Remain in me, and I in you. As the branch can’t bear fruit by itself unless it remains in the vine, so neither can you, unless you remain in me.
>
> **5** I am the vine. You are the branches. He who remains in me and I in him bears much fruit, for apart from me you can do nothing.
>
> **6** If a man doesn’t remain in me, he is thrown out as a branch and is withered; and they gather them, throw them into the fire, and they are burned.
>
> **7** If you remain in me, and my words remain in you, you will ask whatever you desire, and it will be done for you.
>
> **8** “In this my Father is glorified, that you bear much fruit; and so you will be my disciples.
>
> **9** Even as the Father has loved me, I also have loved you. Remain in my love.
>
> **10** If you keep my commandments, you will remain in my love, even as I have kept my Father’s commandments and remain in his love.
>
> **16** You didn’t choose me, but I chose you and appointed you, that you should go and bear fruit, and that your fruit should remain; that whatever you will ask of the Father in my name, he may give it to you.

#### Reading the passages together

- The branch has no life in itself; fruit is the result of communion with Christ through His Word and gifts, not the root of acceptance.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

**Augustine, Confessions 10.29 (Pusey translation, lightly modernized)**

> Give what You command, and command what You will.

*Study significance:* Augustine confesses that divine commands expose need and that God must supply the grace by which they are obeyed.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Augsburg Confession V**

> That we may obtain this faith, the Ministry of Teaching the Gospel and administering the Sacraments was instituted. Through the Word and Sacraments, as through instruments, the Holy Spirit is given, who works faith where and when it pleases God.

**Augsburg Confession VI**

> This faith is bound to bring forth good fruits, and it is necessary to do good works commanded by God because of God's will; yet we should not rely on those works to merit justification before God.

*Study significance:* Good works are necessary fruits of faith, not merits that purchase God's favor.

> Let the one who has love in Christ keep the commandments of Christ.

*Study significance:* The sentence binds Christian love to Christ's actual commands rather than sentiment alone.

**Didache 1.1-2 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> There are two ways, one of life and one of death; but there is a great difference between these two ways. The way of life, then, is this: First, you shall love God who made you; second, your neighbor as yourself; and all things whatsoever you would not have done to you, do not do to another.

*Study significance:* An early Christian catechetical summary joins love of God, love of neighbor, and concrete conduct.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Augsburg Confession IV**

> People cannot be justified before God by their own strength, merits, or works, but are freely justified for Christ's sake, through faith, when they believe that they are received into favor and that their sins are forgiven for Christ's sake.

*Study significance:* The command must never be turned into a competing basis of justification.

**Augsburg Confession VI**

> This faith is bound to bring forth good fruits, and it is necessary to do good works commanded by God because of God's will; yet we should not rely on those works to merit justification before God.

*Study significance:* Good works are necessary fruits of faith, not merits that purchase God's favor.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV and VI (faith and new obedience); Apology of the Augsburg Confession IV (faith and fruits); Small Catechism: First Commandment and Baptism IV; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration IV and VI (good works and the Law).

#### Practicing the command

Learn the commandments, confess failure, receive forgiveness, and pursue concrete obedience in vocation.

#### Misuse caution

Avoid both legalism and antinomianism.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **keep christ's commandments** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to keep christ's commandments. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

### 11. Take Christ's yoke and learn from Him

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #18  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 11:28-30

#### Command-family wording

> Come to Jesus, take His yoke, and learn His gentleness and humility.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 11:28-30

> **28** “Come to me, all you who labor and are heavily burdened, and I will give you rest.
>
> **29** Take my yoke upon you and learn from me, for I am gentle and humble in heart; and you will find rest for your souls.
>
> **30** For my yoke is easy, and my burden is light.”

#### Reading the passages together

- The One who commands also gives rest; His yoke is gracious discipleship, not burdens imposed as conditions of acceptance.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

The One who commands also gives rest; His yoke is gracious discipleship, not burdens imposed as conditions of acceptance. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Augustine, Confessions 1.1 (Pusey translation, lightly modernized)**

> You have made us for Yourself, and our heart is restless until it rests in You.

*Study significance:* The line helps distinguish Christ's promised rest from mere favorable circumstances.

**Augustine, Confessions 10.29 (Pusey translation, lightly modernized)**

> Give what You command, and command what You will.

*Study significance:* Augustine confesses that divine commands expose need and that God must supply the grace by which they are obeyed.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Augsburg Confession IV**

> People cannot be justified before God by their own strength, merits, or works, but are freely justified for Christ's sake, through faith, when they believe that they are received into favor and that their sins are forgiven for Christ's sake.

**Augsburg Confession VI**

3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **take christ's yoke and learn from him** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

### 13. Renounce every rival claim to lordship

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal principle  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #21  
**Canonical passages:** Luke 14:25-33

#### Command-family wording

> Relinquish every possession, relationship, or claim that competes with Christ.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Luke 14:25-33

> **25** Now great multitudes were going with him. He turned and said to them,
>
> **26** “If anyone comes to me, and doesn’t disregard his own father, mother, wife, children, brothers, and sisters, yes, and his own life also, he can’t be my disciple.
>
> **27** Whoever doesn’t bear his own cross and come after me, can’t be my disciple.
>
> **28** For which of you, desiring to build a tower, doesn’t first sit down and count the cost, to see if he has enough to complete it?
>
> **29** Or perhaps, when he has laid a foundation and isn’t able to finish, everyone who sees begins to mock him,
>
> **30** saying, ‘This man began to build and wasn’t able to finish.’
>
> **31** Or what king, as he goes to encounter another king in war, will not sit down first and consider whether he is able with ten thousand to meet him who comes against him with twenty thousand?
>
> **32** Or else, while the other is yet a great way off, he sends an envoy and asks for conditions of peace.
>
> **33** So therefore, whoever of you who doesn’t renounce all that he has, he can’t be my disciple.

#### Reading the passages together

- Jesus exposes idols through stark cost language; all gifts are received back as stewardship under Him.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Jesus exposes idols through stark cost language; all gifts are received back as stewardship under Him. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

**Didache 1.1-2 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

*Study significance:* An early Christian catechetical summary joins love of God, love of neighbor, and concrete conduct.

**1 Clement 49.1 (Lightfoot translation, lightly modernized)**

> Let the one who has love in Christ keep the commandments of Christ.

*Study significance:* The sentence binds Christian love to Christ's actual commands rather than sentiment alone.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Small Catechism, First Commandment**

> We should fear, love, and trust in God above all things.

*Study significance:* Every command family is finally ordered under trust in the true God.

**Large Catechism, First Commandment**

> A god means that from which we are to expect all good and to which we are to take refuge in all distress.

*Study significance:* This exposes whatever receives the trust, fear, or refuge that belongs to God.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV and VI (faith and new obedience); Apology of the Augsburg Confession IV (faith and fruits); Small Catechism: First Commandment and Baptism IV; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration IV and VI (good works and the Law).

Name what is most likely to rule you, hold gifts open-handedly, and refuse sin even when costly.

### 14. Give generously to the poor

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal generosity with individual applications  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #22  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 19:21; Mark 10:21; Luke 12:33; 18:22

#### Command-family wording

> Loosen your grasp on possessions, give to the poor, and treasure Christ above wealth.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 19:21

> **21** Jesus said to him, “If you want to be perfect, go, sell what you have, and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven; and come, follow me.”

##### Mark 10:21

> **21** Jesus looking at him loved him, and said to him, “One thing you lack. Go, sell whatever you have and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven; and come, follow me, taking up the cross.”

##### Luke 12:33

> **33** Sell what you have and give gifts to the needy. Make for yourselves purses which don’t grow old, a treasure in the heavens that doesn’t fail, where no thief approaches and no moth destroys.

##### Luke 18:22

> **22** When Jesus heard these things, he said to him, “You still lack one thing. Sell all that you have and distribute it to the poor. Then you will have treasure in heaven; then come, follow me.”

#### Reading the passages together

- Wealth is a powerful false god; the rich ruler's personal command must be neither universalized into compulsory poverty nor emptied into metaphor.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Wealth is a powerful false god; the rich ruler's personal command must be neither universalized into compulsory poverty nor emptied into metaphor. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Didache 1.5 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> Give to everyone who asks you, and ask it not back; for the Father wills that to all should be given of our own blessings.

*Study significance:* The command is received as real generosity flowing from the Father's gifts.

**1 Clement 49.1 (Lightfoot translation, lightly modernized)**

> Let the one who has love in Christ keep the commandments of Christ.

*Study significance:* The sentence binds Christian love to Christ's actual commands rather than sentiment alone.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Small Catechism, Seventh Commandment**

> We should fear and love God that we may not take our neighbor's money or property, nor get them by false ware or dealing, but help him to improve and protect his property and business.

*Study significance:* Generosity and economic integrity seek the neighbor's material good.

**Augsburg Confession VI**

> This faith is bound to bring forth good fruits, and it is necessary to do good works commanded by God because of God's will; yet we should not rely on those works to merit justification before God.

*Study significance:* Good works are necessary fruits of faith, not merits that purchase God's favor.

**Further confessional anchors:** Augsburg Confession IV and VI (faith and new obedience); Apology of the Augsburg Confession IV (faith and fruits); Small Catechism: First Commandment and Baptism IV; Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration IV and VI (good works and the Law).

#### Practicing the command

Give deliberately, support church and mercy work, simplify where possessions dominate, and protect dependents.

#### Misuse caution

Generosity must not become financial irresponsibility or coercive fundraising.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **give generously to the poor** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to give generously to the poor. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

## Love, Mercy, and Reconciliation

Love of God takes concrete form in mercy, forgiveness, truth, and care for the neighbor.

##### Matthew 22:37-38

> **37** Jesus said to him, “‘You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, with all your soul, and with all your mind.’
>
> **38** This is the first and great commandment.

> **29** Jesus answered, “The greatest is: ‘Hear, Israel, the Lord our God, the Lord is one.
>
> **30** You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, with all your soul, with all your mind, and with all your strength.’ This is the first commandment.

##### Luke 4:8

> **8** Jesus answered him, “Get behind me, Satan! For it is written, ‘You shall worship the Lord your God, and you shall serve him only.’”

### 16. Love your neighbor as yourself

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #24  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 22:39-40; Mark 12:31; Luke 10:27

#### Command-family wording

> Seek your neighbor's good with the seriousness naturally given to your own.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 22:39-40

> **39** A second likewise is this, ‘You shall love your neighbor as yourself.’
>
> **40** The whole law and the prophets depend on these two commandments.”

##### Mark 12:31

> **31** The second is like this: ‘You shall love your neighbor as yourself.’ There is no other commandment greater than these.”

##### Luke 10:27

> **27** He answered, “You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, with all your soul, with all your strength, and with all your mind; and your neighbor as yourself.”

#### Reading the passages together

- Neighbor-love summarizes the second table of the Law and takes shape in protection, truthfulness, chastity, property, and reputation.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Neighbor-love summarizes the second table of the Law and takes shape in protection, truthfulness, chastity, property, and reputation. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Didache 1.1-2 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> There are two ways, one of life and one of death; but there is a great difference between these two ways. The way of life, then, is this: First, you shall love God who made you; second, your neighbor as yourself; and all things whatsoever you would not have done to you, do not do to another.

*Study significance:* An early Christian catechetical summary joins love of God, love of neighbor, and concrete conduct.

**1 Clement 13.1-2 (Lightfoot/Roberts-Donaldson tradition, lightly modernized)**

> Let us be humble-minded, brethren, laying aside all haughtiness and pride and foolishness and angry feelings, especially remembering the words of the Lord Jesus: Be merciful, that you may obtain mercy; forgive, that it may be forgiven unto you.

*Study significance:* Clement explicitly uses Jesus' words to form a peaceful and humble congregation.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Small Catechism, Fifth Commandment**

> We should fear and love God that we may not hurt nor harm our neighbor in his body, but help and befriend him in every bodily need.

*Study significance:* Neighbor-love includes active bodily help, not merely refraining from violence.

**Small Catechism, Eighth Commandment**

> We should fear and love God that we may not deceitfully belie, betray, slander, or defame our neighbor, but defend him, speak well of him, and put the best construction on everything.

*Study significance:* Truthful correction and charitable interpretation belong together.

**Further confessional anchors:** Small Catechism: Fourth through Tenth Commandments; Augsburg Confession VI (new obedience); Augsburg Confession XXVIII (the Church does not invent works that bind consciences); Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration IV (good works as necessary fruits, not merits).

#### Practicing the command

Learn actual needs, serve through vocation, protect life and reputation, and repent of sentimental substitutes for help.

#### Misuse caution

Love can require truthful correction and boundaries; it is not mere approval.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **love your neighbor as yourself** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to love your neighbor as yourself. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

> There are two ways, one of life and one of death; but there is a great difference between these two ways. The way of life, then, is this: First, you shall love God who made you; second, your neighbor as yourself; and all things whatsoever you would not have done to you, do not do to another.

#### Misuse caution

Love does not conceal abuse, suppress doctrine, or protect unrepentant wrongdoing.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **love one another as christ has loved you** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to love one another as christ has loved you. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

<a id="command-018"></a>

### 18. Love enemies and pray for persecutors

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #26, #27  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 5:43-45; Luke 6:27-28,35

#### Command-family wording

> Seek the good of enemies, bless rather than curse, and pray for those who mistreat you.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 5:43-45

> **43** “You have heard that it was said, ‘You shall love your neighbor and hate your enemy.’
>
> **44** But I tell you, love your enemies, bless those who curse you, do good to those who hate you, and pray for those who mistreat you and persecute you,
>
> **45** that you may be children of your Father who is in heaven. For he makes his sun to rise on the evil and the good, and sends rain on the just and the unjust.

##### Luke 6:27-28,35

> **27** “But I tell you who hear: love your enemies, do good to those who hate you,
>
> **28** bless those who curse you, and pray for those who mistreat you.
>
> **35** But love your enemies, and do good, and lend, expecting nothing back; and your reward will be great, and you will be children of the Most High; for he is kind toward the unthankful and evil.

#### Reading the passages together

- Enemy-love entrusts judgment to God while willing repentance, protection, and salvation for the offender; it never calls evil good.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Enemy-love entrusts judgment to God while willing repentance, protection, and salvation for the offender; it never calls evil good. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Didache 1.3 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> Bless those who curse you, and pray for your enemies, and fast for those who persecute you. For what thank is there if you love those who love you? Do not even the Gentiles do the same? But love those who hate you, and you shall not have an enemy.

*Study significance:* The text shows how early Christians heard Jesus' command as prayerful goodwill rather than retaliation.

**Justin Martyr, First Apology 16 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> Concerning our being patient of injuries: Whoever shall strike you on the right cheek, turn to him the other also. And: Let your good works shine before men, that they, seeing them, may glorify your Father who is in heaven.

*Study significance:* The early apologist places non-retaliation and visible good works together as Christian witness.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Small Catechism, Fifth Commandment**

> We should fear and love God that we may not hurt nor harm our neighbor in his body, but help and befriend him in every bodily need.

*Study significance:* Neighbor-love includes active bodily help, not merely refraining from violence.

**Augsburg Confession VI**

> This faith is bound to bring forth good fruits, and it is necessary to do good works commanded by God because of God's will; yet we should not rely on those works to merit justification before God.

*Study significance:* Good works are necessary fruits of faith, not merits that purchase God's favor.

**Further confessional anchors:** Small Catechism: Fourth through Tenth Commandments; Augsburg Confession VI (new obedience); Augsburg Confession XXVIII (the Church does not invent works that bind consciences); Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration IV (good works as necessary fruits, not merits).

#### Practicing the command

Pray by name, refuse revenge and dehumanizing speech, use lawful means to restrain harm, and offer kindness when safe.

#### Misuse caution

Forgiveness and love do not require unsafe access or abandonment of justice.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **love enemies and pray for persecutors** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

Lord Jesus Christ, You call Your people to love enemies and pray for persecutors. Forgive my failure, keep me in Your Word and gifts, and bear this fruit in me for the good of my neighbor and the glory of the Father. Amen.

### 19. Do to others as you would have them do to you

**Corpus:** Gospels  
**Scope:** Universal  
**Earlier-list crosswalk:** #28  
**Canonical passages:** Matthew 7:12; Luke 6:31

#### Command-family wording

> Act toward others with the fairness, mercy, and initiative you would rightly desire.

#### Scripture text (World English Bible)

##### Matthew 7:12

> **12** Therefore, whatever you desire for men to do to you, you shall also do to them; for this is the law and the prophets.

##### Luke 6:31

> **31** “As you would like people to do to you, do exactly so to them.

#### Reading the passages together

- The Golden Rule states neighbor-love positively: disciples do not merely avoid harm but imagine and pursue good.

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

The Golden Rule states neighbor-love positively: disciples do not merely avoid harm but imagine and pursue good. In a confessional Lutheran reading, this command is not a ladder by which sinners earn justification. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. The command must therefore be preached in the whole counsel of God: with Christ's completed saving work, the means by which He gives His gifts, the distinction between justification and sanctification, and the Christian's concrete vocations.

#### Early Christian witnesses

**Didache 1.1-2 (Roberts-Donaldson translation, lightly modernized)**

> There are two ways, one of life and one of death; but there is a great difference between these two ways. The way of life, then, is this: First, you shall love God who made you; second, your neighbor as yourself; and all things whatsoever you would not have done to you, do not do to another.

*Study significance:* An early Christian catechetical summary joins love of God, love of neighbor, and concrete conduct.

**1 Clement 13.1-2 (Lightfoot/Roberts-Donaldson tradition, lightly modernized)**

> Let us be humble-minded, brethren, laying aside all haughtiness and pride and foolishness and angry feelings, especially remembering the words of the Lord Jesus: Be merciful, that you may obtain mercy; forgive, that it may be forgiven unto you.

*Study significance:* Clement explicitly uses Jesus' words to form a peaceful and humble congregation.

#### Lutheran confessional witnesses

**Small Catechism, Fifth Commandment**

> We should fear and love God that we may not hurt nor harm our neighbor in his body, but help and befriend him in every bodily need.

*Study significance:* Neighbor-love includes active bodily help, not merely refraining from violence.

**Small Catechism, Eighth Commandment**

> We should fear and love God that we may not deceitfully belie, betray, slander, or defame our neighbor, but defend him, speak well of him, and put the best construction on everything.

*Study significance:* Truthful correction and charitable interpretation belong together.

**Further confessional anchors:** Small Catechism: Fourth through Tenth Commandments; Augsburg Confession VI (new obedience); Augsburg Confession XXVIII (the Church does not invent works that bind consciences); Formula of Concord, Solid Declaration IV (good works as necessary fruits, not merits).

#### Practicing the command

Reverse positions before deciding, seek informed understanding, repair harms, and apply the rule across vocations.

#### Misuse caution

God's Word, not personal preference, defines what one ought to desire.

#### Questions for examination and meditation

1. What exactly does Jesus command, forbid, promise, or warn in these passages, and who first receives the words?
2. Which words expose sin or false trust, and which words direct you to Christ's gift, forgiveness, or sustaining promise?
3. What concrete act of repentance, faith, mercy, or vocation would embody **do to others as you would have them do to you** this week without turning obedience into a basis of justification?

#### Prayer

> **7** But if you had known what this means, ‘I desire mercy, and not sacrifice,’ you wouldn’t have condemned the guiltless.

##### Luke 6:36

#### Reading the passages together

#### Law, Gospel, and confessional synthesis

Mercy moves toward misery with help and forgiveness while revealing the loving purpose of God's moral will. The Law exposes sin and directs the baptized life; the Gospel forgives, creates faith, joins the believer to Christ, and brings forth the fruits named here. **1 Clement 13.1-2 (Lightfoot/Roberts-Donaldson tradition, lightly modernized)**

> Let us be humble-minded, brethren, laying aside all haughtiness and pride and foolishness and angry feelings, especially remembering the words of the Lord Jesus: Be merciful, that you may obtain mercy; forgive, that it may be forgiven unto you.

**Small Catechism, Fifth Commandment**

> We should fear and love God that we may not hurt nor harm our neighbor in his body, but help and befriend him in every bodily need.

Attend to suffering, give practical aid, interpret charitably, and let received absolution make you patient.
